[
  {

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n2\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F32.4, F32.5, F32.8, F32.9, F33.0, F33.1, F33.2, F33.3, F33.4, F33.8, F33.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess what brought me in is... I just don't really feel like myself anymore. I barely talk to anyone lately. I just kind of go flat and quiet. And at work—I'm a building cleaner—even just getting started on my cleaning route feels like pushing a loaded cart uphill. I just don't have it in me.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. Doing them just doesn't give me anything back anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep patterns lately?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. My sleep has been pretty much the same as it always is. I just go to bed and wake up for my shift like usual. I haven't noticed any real changes there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. Most days... food just kind of tastes like cardboard to me now. I end up skipping meals, or maybe I'll just eat a few crackers after my shift is over. I guess I'm losing weight from it, too, because my work pants have been hanging a lot looser on me than they used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving more slowly than you used to, or having trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I'm moving around at work, pushing the mop and doing my rounds at about the same pace I always do. I haven't noticed feeling slowed down or anything. And I don't have any trouble just sitting still, either.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts that you've let people down or that you're a failure?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. I just... I keep thinking I failed everybody by losing my old place and not keeping my life together like I should have. It weighs on me a lot. It even gets to the point where, if the church pantry is short on supplies, or even if a hallway just gets missed at work... I somehow feel like that's on me, too. Like it's my fault.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't have some big plan to do it or anything. But... yeah, a lot of mornings I wake up and just think that it might be easier if I just didn't wake up at all. And honestly, having those thoughts... it scares me enough to actually say it out loud to you.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Well, the depression part... it's been with me for about a year and two months now. It started up not long after I had to move.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I used to look forward to a few things, like going fishing on Saturdays, or just sitting with a cup of coffee and doing the crossword puzzle. Doing them just doesn't give me anything back anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep patterns lately?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. My sleep has been pretty much the same as it always is. I just go to bed and wake up for my shift like usual. So, no, I don't really get any enjoyment out of much right now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small things that still bring you a tiny bit of joy or happiness?\n\nPatient: Like I was just saying... no, not really. Even the small stuff. I used to look forward to getting my coffee and doing the crossword, or going fishing on Saturdays. Or even just showing the younger guys at work some tricks for stripping a floor. But I mostly just let all that sit now. None of it gives me anything back anymore. So, no, I don't really get even a tiny bit of joy out of things right now.\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you think of any activity that you used to enjoy, but now you don't, and if so, did you lose interest in it suddenly or was it a gradual process?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 59 / janitor_or_building_cleaner / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Loss_of_Interest\nmanifestation: \"I used to look forward to fishing on Saturdays, doing crosswords with coffee, or showing the younger guys tricks for stripping a floor, but now I mostly let those things sit because they don’t give me anything back.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, like I was just saying, it's exactly those things I mentioned. Fishing on Saturdays, doing the crosswords with my coffee, or showing the younger guys at work how to strip a floor. I used to really look forward to all of that. 

As to whether it was sudden or gradual... I guess I didn't really wake up one day and just stop. It just kind of faded out. I just started letting those things sit more and more, because... well, like I said, they just don't give me anything back anymore.
